\paragraph{The escape, as a recipe---and this, not the criterion, is the practical contribution.} Hold $d_2$, the attack, and therefore the gate's input $M(d_2,a)$ fixed; intervene on the upstream transform; and \textbf{measure the channel rather than the outcome-conditional contrast}---for us, the admitted adversarial mass rather than the certified-versus-uncertified difference. What an evaluation must hold fixed to license a causal claim about mechanism is precisely what Proposition~\ref{prop:identification}'s hypothesis enumerates, and it is what we would ask a reader to carry into their own evaluation.

Read this way the boundary is not an apology for a failed suite but a design derivation, and §\ref{sec:dose_response} runs the design it licenses: the same downstream defense, the same attack, the same standalone effectiveness, and a controlled, independently measured dose of disturbance. Figure~\ref{fig:mechanism} sets out the mechanism the criterion checks and the measured chain for FG$\to$RFA.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/mechanism_chain.pdf}
\caption{\textbf{The criterion is a mechanism test, and the mechanism is measurable.}
\textbf{(a)} What C2 checks: the upstream transform $T$ and the statistic $S$ that $d_2$ reads
determine an \emph{invariance class} (Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}), and the class
determines the verdict---no composition needs to be run. This is why norm clipping does not
blind FoolsGold but does blind Reputation.
\textbf{(b)} The measured chain for FG$\to$RFA, per round, on the transformed points RFA
actually receives. Each marker is one round with $w_a>0$ (rounds where FoolsGold zeroes every
adversary are Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} and carry no payload); large markers are
mean~$\pm$~s.d. Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass}'s bound holds in every round, and the
$\alpha{=}0.1$ collapse is visible as a collapse in the theorem's own quantity: $m$ falls from
$4.71 \pm 1.51$ ($n{=}17$ rounds) to $2.06 \pm 2.35$ ($n{=}12$), with several rounds at
$m{<}1$ where the bound degrades to $n_a/(n_b{+}n_a)$, and realized $\Lambda_a$ rises from
$0.003$ to $0.085$ (max $0.50$) as composition ASR goes $0.093 \to 0.569$. Values below
$10^{-4}$ are drawn at $10^{-4}$.}
\label{fig:mechanism}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A second boundary: what such a screen cannot find.} The gate that limits what a screen can be \emph{validated} on also limits what it can \emph{cover}---which converts the most obvious objection to our criterion, that its recall is poor, into a scope result.

\begin{proposition}[Inherited-suppression screens cannot certify emergent synergy]
\label{prop:emergent}
Call $(d_1 \to d_2)$'s suppression of $a$ \textbf{emergent} if $M(d_1 \to d_2, a) < \theta$ while $\min_{d \in \{d_1,d_2\}} M(d,a) \ge \theta$. No emergent composition satisfies the eligibility gate of Proposition~\ref{prop:identification}. Hence, writing $\mathcal{S}$ for the compositions with $M < \theta$ and $\mathcal{E} \subseteq \mathcal{S}$ for the emergent ones, any such screen's recall obeys $\mathrm{recall} \le 1 - |\mathcal{E}|/|\mathcal{S}|$ on every menu and every attack.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Emergence is the negation of the gate, so each member of $\mathcal{E}$ is rejected while belonging to $\mathcal{S}$: a false negative. The bound is the resulting recall.
\end{proof}

\noindent \textbf{This is a scope theorem, not a defect found by tuning}, and it is specific neither to C1 nor to FL: any screen requiring a constituent to suppress the attack independently inherits it. Our own lowest-ASR composition is the witness---FG$\to$RFA reaches $0.093$ where FoolsGold alone reaches $0.732$ and RFA alone $0.845$---so the bound binds at $|\mathcal{E}|/|\mathcal{S}| = 1/5$, which is exactly the $40\%$ recall we report (§\ref{sec:condition_scoring}, §\ref{sec:fg_rfa_flagship}). Screening for inherited robustness and searching for synergistic compositions are different problems; only the first is ours.

\paragraph{Mechanism--effectiveness confounding: Proposition~\ref{prop:identification} in our instance.} The boundary has an uncomfortable consequence for validation, and it is worth spelling out concretely. In \emph{every} in-scope pair the criterion certifies, $d_2$ already suppresses the attack that $d_1$ fails. A certified composition's low ASR can therefore always be attributed to $d_2$'s standalone strength (C1) rather than to $d_1$ having preserved $d_2$'s mechanism (C2, C3): the two are confounded \textbf{by the conditions themselves}, not by an unlucky choice of defense menu. We name this \textbf{mechanism--effectiveness confounding} and offer it as a methodological observation about validating composition criteria of this shape, applicable beyond our own experiments.

In particular it rules out the experiment we previously named as decisive: a $d_2$ whose admission signal is invariant under positive rescaling but which is \emph{weak in isolation} cannot satisfy C1 in scope, so no such certified pair exists to test. The achievable substitute is \textbf{per-attack complementarity}---a $d_2$ that suppresses the attack $d_1$ fails while remaining weak on a \emph{different} committed attack, so its max-committed ASR stays high and it cannot suppress the composition single-handedly. Reputation is such a defense ($0.017$ on model-scaling, $0.842$ on the pixel backdoor), and §\ref{sec:metric_swap} builds the test on that observation.
\begin{proof}[Argument sketch]
(C1 sufficient) If at least one of $d_1$, $d_2$ individually suppresses $a$, composition inherits that suppression provided C2$\wedge$C3 preserve the signal chain. C1 is sufficient, not necessary: two individually-weak defenses may suppress synergistically through complementary mechanisms (e.g., partial downweighting + geometric rejection), but predicting such synergy requires the quantitative margins below. The criterion is therefore \emph{conservative}---it may reject working compositions but will not approve broken ones.

(C2 role) Suppose $d_2$ individually suppresses $a$ via signal $\sigma_2(u_a) > \theta$. If $d_1$'s transformation $T_1$ destroys this signal ($\sigma_2(T_1(u_a)) \not\approx \sigma_2(u_a)$), $d_2$ cannot detect the attack. Example: NormClip as $d_1$ equalizes all norms to $\leq\tau$, so reputation's distance signal $\sigma_2(u) = \|u - \hat\mu\|$ collapses---adversarial and benign updates become equidistant from consensus. Quantitatively: C2 holds when relative signal distortion $\Delta_{\mathrm{signal}} := |\sigma_2(T_1(u_a)) - \sigma_2(u_a)|/\sigma_2(u_a) < \delta_2$ for a defense-specific tolerance $\delta_2$.

(C3 role) Even when $d_1$ preserves the \emph{direction} of $d_2$'s signal (C2 holds), it may compress magnitudes below $d_2$'s threshold. Example: FoolsGold as $d_1$ downweights adversarial clients by $\alpha \ll 1$. TrimmedMean's coordinate-wise rank signal still distinguishes adversarial coordinates (C2 holds), but after rescaling by $\alpha$, per-coordinate values $\alpha \cdot u_{a,k}$ fall within the trimming range---the coordinate-wise extreme that would trigger trimming is no longer extreme. Quantitatively: C3 holds when the margin ratio $\Delta_{\mathrm{margin}} := (\tau_{d_2}(T_1(u_a)) - \theta)/(\tau_{d_2}(u_a) - \theta) > 0$---i.e., adversarial scores remain above $d_2$'s rejection boundary after $d_1$'s transform.

(Sufficiency) If C1 holds, C2 holds ($\Delta_{\mathrm{signal}} < \delta_2$), and C3 holds ($\Delta_{\mathrm{margin}} > 0$), then $d_2$ detects and suppresses $u_a$ even after $d_1$'s transformation. For the weighting$\to$aggregator class, Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight} instantiates these margins explicitly: $\rho < r^\star$ (rank) or $\rho < n_b/n_a$ (geometric). \qedhere
\end{proof}

\subsection{A restricted theorem: C2$\wedge$C3 for weighting$\to$rank and weighting$\to$geometric compositions}
\label{sec:restricted_theorem}

\noindent\textbf{What follows is a boundary theorem, and deliberately not a security theorem.} It bounds what an \emph{invariance} argument about composition can establish---preservation of the downstream defense's discriminative mechanism, levels (P1)--(P3) of Definition~\ref{def:levels}---and it says nothing whatever about suppression, which lives at (P4)--(P5). We state that in the same breath as the theorem because the whole point of the paper is the gap between the two: the theorem is the best case for statistic-level reasoning, proved with explicit margins, and §\ref{sec:targeted} then shows that even exact invariance neither implies nor preserves suppression. Read it as a \emph{formal} claim in the sense of Table~\ref{tab:scope_levels}: proved, on one class, bounding no ASR.

The criterion above is an operational heuristic. For the class of compositions that matter most in practice---a per-client \emph{weighting} defense $d_1$ (reputation, FoolsGold) followed by a \emph{rank-based} or \emph{geometric} aggregator $d_2$ (TrimmedMean, CoordMedian, RFA), which contains the flagship FoolsGold$\to$RFA---C2 and C3 can be \emph{proven} rather than asserted, with an explicit tolerance on how aggressively $d_1$ may reweight. The key structural fact is that $d_2$'s admission score is scale-equivariant, so it commutes with $d_1$'s reweighting up to a bounded ratio; the composition fails only when $d_1$ compresses that ratio past a margin set by $d_2$'s separation.

\begin{theorem}[Bounded reweighting preserves the downstream discriminative mechanism]
\label{thm:bounded_reweight}
Consider a round with $n_b$ benign and $n_a$ adversarial updates under honest majority $n_b > n_a$. Let $d_1$ be a per-client reweighting $T_1(u_i) = w_i u_i$ with $w_i \geq 0$, and define the ratio over \emph{strictly positive} weights, $\rho := \max_i w_i / \min_{i:\,w_i>0} w_i$. Suppose $d_2$ suppresses attack $a$ through a client admission score $\sigma_2(\cdot\,;U)$ that is scale-equivariant, $\sigma_2(c\,u_i; c\,U) = c^{k}\sigma_2(u_i;U)$ for all $c>0$ and some degree $k\ge 0$. Assume $w_a > 0$ for adversarial clients; the $w_a = 0$ case is Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation}. The theorem establishes preservation of $d_2$'s suppression \emph{mechanism}; it does not bound ASR. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{(Rank aggregators; C2$\wedge$C3 hold up to a margin.)} If $d_2\in\{\text{TrimmedMean},\text{CoordMedian}\}$, so $\sigma_2$ is a coordinate-wise rank, then $d_2$'s per-coordinate suppression of $a$ is preserved when two conditions hold. \textbf{(i)~Count majority:} $n_a < K/2$ for CoordMedian, and $n_a \leq \lfloor\beta K\rfloor$ for TrimmedMean with $\beta$-trimming. \textbf{(ii)~Signed ordering:} on each suppression-relevant coordinate $k$, the adversarial transformed value lies strictly beyond every benign one, $w_a u_{a,k} > \max_b w_b u_{b,k}$ (upper tail) or $w_a u_{a,k} < \min_b w_b u_{b,k}$ (lower tail). \emph{Sufficient magnitude condition for} (ii): if $\mathrm{sign}(u_{a,k})$ is common to all adversaries and $|u_{a,k}| > \rho\cdot\max_b|u_{b,k}|$, then (ii) holds; writing $r^\star := \min_k \min_b |u_{a,k}|/|u_{b,k}|$ for the coordinate-wise separation margin, this is $\rho < r^\star$. Magnitude alone is \emph{not} sufficient without the sign assumption: an adversarial coordinate can be large in absolute value yet sit between the benign values. At $\rho=1$ ranks are preserved exactly, recovering Proposition~\ref{prop:rank_invariance}.
\item \textbf{(Geometric median / RFA; C2$\wedge$C3 hold up to a margin.)} The composition hands RFA the \emph{transformed points} $u'_i := w_i u_i$ and RFA runs its standard \emph{unweighted} geometric median on them---it is not a weighted geometric median, and $w_i$ enters only by moving the points. All quantities must therefore be defined on the transformed set. Let $\mu'_B$ be the geometric median of the benign $u'_b$ alone, $\mu'$ that of all of $U'$, $R'_B := \max_b\|u'_b - \mu'_B\|$ the transformed benign radius, and $\delta' := \|\mu' - \mu'_B\|$ the displacement caused by including the adversarial points. Suppression is preserved when
\[
\Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}} := \min_{a,b}\|u'_a - u'_b\| \;>\; 2\,(R'_B + \delta'),
\]
under which every adversarial residual $\|u'_a - \mu'\|$ strictly exceeds every benign residual $\|u'_b - \mu'\|$, so RFA's inverse-residual weight on $a$ stays below the benign floor.
\item \textbf{(Norm-equalizing $d_1$; C2 fails---the degenerate case.)} If instead $d_1$ maps every client norm to a common value (NormClip with $\tau$ below all client norms), then any $\sigma_2$ keyed on update norm or consensus distance becomes constant across clients, so $\sigma_2$ no longer separates $a$ and C2 fails. This is \emph{not} a bounded reweighting: the induced per-coordinate weight ratio is unbounded, so no $\rho$-margin applies.
\end{enumerate}
Parts (1) and (2) are sufficient conditions for $d_2$ to \emph{preserve the downstream suppression ordering}---not to exclude adversarial contributions outright. Neither is a sufficient condition for low ASR, and part (2) orders residuals per client only---it constrains the \emph{aggregate} adversarial coefficient solely through Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(1) TrimmedMean/CoordMedian act on coordinate $k$ through the rank of $w_i u_{i,k}$ among $\{w_j u_{j,k}\}_j$; $a$ is suppressed on $k$ because its transformed coordinate is extreme (trimmed, or not selected by the median). Given (ii), at most $n_a$ of the extreme order statistics are adversarial, so with (i) the selected value is benign. For the magnitude condition, $w_a>0$ gives $\rho \ge \max_b w_b/w_a$, so $|u_{a,k}| > \rho\max_b|u_{b,k}|$ implies $|w_a u_{a,k}| > w_a\rho\max_b|u_{b,k}| \ge (\max_b w_b)\max_b|u_{b,k}| \ge \max_b|w_b u_{b,k}|$; with a sign common to all adversaries this places $w_a u_{a,k}$ strictly beyond every benign value on that side. Scale-equivariance ($k{=}0$ for pure rank) makes the uniform case $\rho{=}1$ exact.
(2) All quantities are on the transformed points $u'_i = w_i u_i$, which is what RFA receives. Every benign residual obeys $\|u'_b - \mu'\| \le R'_B + \delta'$ by the triangle inequality. For adversarial $u'_a$, $\|u'_a - \mu'_B\| \ge \Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}} - R'_B$, hence $\|u'_a - \mu'\| \ge \Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}} - R'_B - \delta'$. Demanding that this exceed the benign bound gives $\Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}} - R'_B - \delta' > R'_B + \delta'$, i.e.\ the stated condition. No weighted-majority assumption is needed, and none would be appropriate: RFA weights every input point equally and the $w_i$ act only by relocating the points. Our implementation runs Weiszfeld for 50 iterations at tolerance $10^{-6}$, so the argument transfers to it.
(3) Clipping to a common $\tau$ sends $\|u_i\|\mapsto\tau$ for all $i$, so $\sigma_2(u_i)=\|u_i\|$ (NormClip-as-$d_2$) or the consensus distance $\|u_i-\hat\mu\|$ collapses to a client-independent constant; separation vanishes, which is precisely C2-failure. \qedhere
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(2) orders residuals \emph{per client}: each adversarial point receives less inverse-residual weight than each benign point. That alone does not bound the adversary's \emph{total} share of the aggregate---several adversaries could each be down-weighted yet collectively hold substantial mass. The separation margin controls this, but only quantitatively:

\begin{corollary}[Bounded aggregate adversarial influence]
\label{cor:aggregate_mass}
Let $m := \Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}}/[2(R'_B+\delta')]$ be the realized separation margin, so Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(2) reads $m>1$. Every benign residual is at most $D_b := R'_B+\delta'$ and every adversarial residual at least $D_a := \Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}}-R'_B-\delta' = (2m-1)D_b$. Bounding $\sum_j 1/d_j$ below by the benign terms alone and each adversarial term above by $1/D_a$,
\[
\Lambda_a \;=\; \sum_{a}\lambda_a \;\leq\; \frac{n_a/D_a}{n_b/D_b + n_a/D_a} \;=\; \frac{n_a}{n_b(2m-1)+n_a},
\]
so $\mu'$ is a convex combination whose adversarial share is at most $\Lambda_a$: writing $\mu' = (1-\Lambda_a)\bar u_B + \Lambda_a \bar u_A$ for its benign and adversarial parts, the displacement from the benign-only combination $\bar u_B$ is at most $\Lambda_a\|\bar u_A - \bar u_B\|$.
\end{corollary}

\noindent The bound is only as good as the margin, and \textbf{the separation condition alone is not enough}: as $m\to1$ it degrades to $n_a/(n_b+n_a)$, permitting the adversary $40\%$ of the mass at $n_a{=}2$, $n_b{=}3$. A margin comfortably above $1$ does the work. We measure $m \geq 2.82$ in every round with $w_a>0$ (mean $4.71$ over those rounds), capping the adversarial share at $5.1\%$ for $n_a{=}1$ and $12.6\%$ for $n_a{=}2$. The realized figure is far tighter than the bound: measuring $\Lambda_a$ directly, it never exceeds $1.8\%$ in any round, and the bound is satisfied in all 17 measured rounds with $w_a>0$. With Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} this completes the picture, and the mechanism survives for \emph{opposite} reasons: at $w_a{=}0$ the adversaries hold most of the Weiszfeld mass ($\Lambda_a=0.71$ measured) but carry no payload; at $w_a>0$ they carry payload but hold at most $12.6\%$.

\noindent\textbf{This is the one place in the paper where the theory predicts a number and the measurement lands inside it}, so we state the pair plainly rather than leaving it in an appendix: \textbf{bound $12.6\%$, realized $\le 1.8\%$}, in every measured round with $w_a>0$. \textbf{And it is also the one place where heterogeneity makes the guarantee measurably empty.} The bound is a function of the realized margin $m$ alone, and at Dirichlet $\alpha{=}0.1$ RFA's \textbf{all-round mean} margin \textbf{collapses from $m{=}3.24$ to $m{=}1.01$}---exactly the limit at which Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass} degrades to $n_a/(n_b{+}n_a)$ and certifies nothing---with two of three certified compositions crossing the ASR threshold there (§\ref{sec:oos_pass}(E)). So the theorem's explanatory value is genuine and measured in \emph{both} directions: it predicts a number that holds where heterogeneity is moderate, and its own quantity records where it stops holding. \textbf{That is not a robustness check; it is one of the paper's three central empirical findings} (§\ref{sec:targeted}, §\ref{sec:conclusion}).

\paragraph{$\Lambda_a$ is level (P4) in measurable form---but (P4) is a \emph{level}, not a single metric.} Before the instantiation, one clarification the rest of the paper depends on: \textbf{(P4) is a conceptual level of the hierarchy and is instantiated defense-specifically}, because different aggregator families have different notions of what it means for adversarial input to enter the aggregate at all. A \emph{selector} admits or does not, so its (P4) is an indicator in $\{0,1\}$; a \emph{weighted averager} has an adversarial coefficient share in $[0,1]$; a \emph{coordinate-wise order statistic} has a fraction of selected coordinates that are adversarial. There is no single scalar all three compute, and this is why the influence column of Table~\ref{tab:channels} is \textbf{necessarily aggregator-specific} rather than a common measurement across rows---a fact about the level, not a shortcoming of the table. With that said: Definition~\ref{def:levels}'s levels are not equally meaningful. (P1)--(P3) are properties of a statistic and of a decision rule reading it; \textbf{(P4) is the first level whose content is the adversary's influence on the aggregate}, and for a geometric aggregator $\Lambda_a$ is exactly that level made measurable---Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass} bounds it, Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} drives it to a benign-only convex combination, and §\ref{sec:targeted}'s instruments intervene on it directly. So the negative results do not leave the theory empty-handed: \textbf{the level the evidence points to is one the theory can bound---just not from a $(d_1,d_2)$ definition pair alone}, which is what a screen would need. That gap is the open problem we state in §\ref{sec:conclusion}. \textbf{Three quantities are distinguished throughout and never used interchangeably}: \emph{admitted adversarial mass}---whether an adversarial update enters $d_2$'s aggregate at all, binary for a selector and a coordinate fraction for an order statistic; the \emph{adversarial coefficient share}, the fraction of the upstream coefficient vector's total mass held by adversaries, which is what the instruments of §\ref{sec:targeted} pin; and \emph{aggregate adversarial influence} $\Lambda_a$, the weight the adversary's direction actually carries in the aggregate. Unqualified, ``admission'' means the first. (Symbols are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:notation}.)

\begin{lemma}[Zero-weight annihilation]
\label{lem:annihilation}
Zero-weight clients are \emph{not} removed from the composition: $d_2$ receives them as zero vectors and still sees all $K$ points. Our implementation runs the $\varepsilon$-smoothed Weiszfeld iteration ($\varepsilon=10^{-8}$), whose fixed point is $\mu' = \sum_i \lambda_i u'_i$ with $\lambda_i \propto 1/\max(\|u'_i-\mu'\|,\varepsilon)$---a convex combination for \emph{every} configuration, including the singular case $\mu'\in U'$ that a zero-weight client at the origin can create, so no non-degeneracy assumption is required. If $w_a = 0$ for every adversarial client, then $u'_a = 0$ and, writing $\Lambda_a := \sum_{a}\lambda_a$, the adversarial terms vanish identically:
$\mu' = \sum_{b\text{ benign}}\lambda_b u'_b = (1-\Lambda_a)\,\bar u$ with $\bar u$ a convex combination of benign transformed updates. The aggregate is a \emph{contraction by $1-\Lambda_a\in(0,1)$ of a benign-only weighted average}: the attack direction never enters $\mu'$. The origin points' only effect is that contraction---a reduction in effective step size, i.e.\ a utility cost, not a security failure. The same holds for CoordMedian, where a zeroed coordinate carries no payload but shrinks the aggregate when selected.
\end{lemma}

\noindent\textbf{Why the positive-weight restriction matters.} Defining $\rho$ over all weights would make it \emph{infinite in every round}: FoolsGold's normalization subtracts the minimum weight, so 2 of 5 clients receive exactly zero (measured, $N{=}10$, $K{=}5$). A bound of the form $\rho < 4$ would then never be satisfiable, and Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(2) would be vacuous on the very composition it is stated over. Restricting $\rho$ to strictly positive weights and handling $w_a{=}0$ separately via Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} is what makes the analysis apply to the algorithm we actually run.

\noindent\textbf{FG$\to$RFA is case 2, and this is where its suppression comes from.} FoolsGold$\to$RFA instantiates Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(2) together with Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation}, and the two are exhaustive. Measuring on the transformed points RFA actually receives (25 adversary-present rounds, 3 seeds): FoolsGold zeroes every adversary in 32\% of rounds, where Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} gives suppression with a measured contraction $1-\Lambda_a = 0.29$ on average; in the remaining 68\% the separation condition holds in \emph{every} round, with margin $\Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}}/2(R'_B+\delta') \ge 2.8$ (mean $3.2$) and $\delta'/R'_B$ mean $0.19$. Suppression is therefore derived rather than merely observed, under assumptions we verify rather than assert. The contraction is also the source of the composition's accuracy cost (0.50 for FG$\to$RFA against 0.78 for RFA alone). The families covered here span the criterion's PASS mechanism, its C3-margin misses, and its C2-failure mode; remaining defenses are empirically-validated extensions (§\ref{sec:composability_validation}).

\noindent\textbf{The margin behaves like a certificate, not just a switch.} C3 is stated as a binary test, but the margin $m$ is measurable and its \emph{strength} tracks security. Across the 29 measured rounds with $w_a>0$ spanning both heterogeneity settings, higher margin accompanies lower realized adversarial mass (Spearman $\rho=-0.48$, $p=0.009$), and Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass}'s bound holds in every one. The same ordering appears at composition level: $m=4.71 \Rightarrow \Lambda_a=0.003 \Rightarrow$ ASR $0.093$ ($\alpha{=}0.5$) versus $m=2.06 \Rightarrow \Lambda_a=0.085 \Rightarrow$ ASR $0.569$ ($\alpha{=}0.1$). That is what makes the condition operationally useful---a short pilot returns a number rather than a verdict---but the composition-level link rests on two heterogeneity settings only, so we report it as directional rather than fitted.

\noindent\textbf{Connecting margins to predictions.} The theorem's conditions give computable margins per pair, but they are met in \emph{two} distinct ways, so reporting $\rho$ alone mispredicts. Let $r := w_a/\max_b w_b$ be the adversary's relative weight and $S$ the attack's coordinate extremeness. \textbf{Regime A (ordering preservation)}, $S\cdot r > 1$: the adversary stays extreme and the rank aggregator discards it---FoolsGold sits here, with bounded weights, $\rho \le 2.1$, hence $r \ge 0.48$ and $S\cdot r \ge 4.8$ at $S{=}10$. \textbf{Regime B (attenuation)}, $S\cdot r \ll 1$: the adversarial transformed value falls \emph{inside} the benign spread, where it cannot be an extreme order statistic and its payload is scaled by $r$; $r{=}0$ is the exact annihilation of Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation}. Reputation sits here, emphatically so: its exponential weights $\exp(-d_i/\sigma)$ give $\rho$ mean $1.4\times10^{5}$ and max $2.8\times10^{6}$, with measured $w_a = 4.8\times10^{-4}$ against benign $0.278$, hence $r = 1.7\times10^{-3}$ and $S\cdot r = 0.017$. Consequently \textbf{reputation$\to$CoordMedian---a PASS pair---violates the bounded-ratio condition $\rho < r^\star$ by roughly five orders of magnitude and still suppresses}, because a huge $\rho$ here signals that reputation is \emph{crushing} the adversary rather than failing to separate it. Both regimes preserve the mechanism; the hazard is the middle, $S\cdot r \approx 1$, where the adversary is neither discarded nor attenuated. C3-FAIL pairs land near that middle or exceed their count budget (FoolsGold$\to$TrimmedMean below); C2-FAIL pairs have $\Delta_{\mathrm{signal}} \to \infty$ under a non-invariant signal (Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}(c)).

\noindent\textbf{Operational calibration protocol.} To evaluate the criterion for ($d_1{\to}d_2$): \textbf{C1} is available from standard single-defense baselines (zero cost). \textbf{C2/C3 for the theorem-backed class} (FG or reputation as $d_1$; RFA, CM, TM as $d_2$): Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}'s bound $\rho < n_b/n_a$ is checkable from algorithm properties alone---no calibration runs needed. \textbf{C2/C3 for other families}: one short pilot (5--10 rounds, ${\sim}10\%$ of full cost) measuring $\sigma_2(T_1(u_a))$ vs.\ $\sigma_2(u_a)$ suffices. The full 50-round experiment runs only for criterion-PASS candidates. $\delta_2 = 0.5$ separates all PASS from C2-FAIL cases on the development set; insensitive to choice: any $\delta_2 \in [0.3, 0.7]$ yields identical classifications on all 42 pairs.

\begin{proposition}[Rank-invariance of coordinate-wise aggregators]
\label{prop:rank_invariance}
Let $d_2$ be a coordinate-wise rank aggregator (TrimmedMean or CoordMedian) with admission statistic $\sigma_k(u) = \mathrm{rank}_k(u)$ among the received updates $\{u_1, \ldots, u_m\}$. Let $T_1(u_i) = \alpha \cdot u_i$ be a \emph{uniform} positive rescaling ($\alpha > 0$, same for all clients). Then:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
\item $\sigma_k(T_1(u_i)) = \sigma_k(u_i)$ for all $i, k$ --- i.e., C2 and C3 hold trivially.
\item Under \emph{heterogeneous} rescaling $T_1(u_i) = \alpha_i \cdot u_i$ with $\alpha_i \neq \alpha_j$, rank preservation fails when rescaling reorders coordinates across clients: $\exists\, k$ s.t.\ $\mathrm{rank}_k(\alpha_i u_{i,k}) \neq \mathrm{rank}_k(u_{i,k})$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(1) Rank is invariant to positive scalar multiplication: $u_{i,k} > u_{j,k} \iff \alpha u_{i,k} > \alpha u_{j,k}$ for $\alpha > 0$. (2) Counterexample: let $u_{1,k} = 3$, $u_{2,k} = 2$, with $\alpha_1 = 0.1$, $\alpha_2 = 1$. Then $\alpha_1 u_{1,k} = 0.3 < \alpha_2 u_{2,k} = 2$, reversing the rank.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor:cm_robust}
CoordMedian (pure rank selection) is robust to C3 boundary compression under any uniform rescaling but vulnerable under heterogeneous client-specific rescaling (e.g., FoolsGold or reputation weights). This predicts that compositions X$\to$CoordMedian will show \emph{intermediate} ASR when $d_1$ applies heterogeneous weights---consistent with observed NC$\to$cm (0.433) and rfa$\to$cm (0.485), both near the 0.5 threshold.
\end{corollary}

Proposition~\ref{prop:rank_invariance} concerns rank under \emph{uniform} rescaling. The sharper question is which admission signals survive \emph{heterogeneous} per-client rescaling, since that is what every per-client defense in our menu applies---NormClip ($c_i = \min(1,\tau/\|u_i\|)$), reputation ($c_i \propto e^{-d_i/s}$) and RFA-reweighting ($c_i \propto 1/\max(\|u_i-\mu'\|,\varepsilon)$) are all of the form $T(u_i) = c_i u_i$ with $c_i > 0$ strictly, each positive by construction. FoolsGold is of the same form with $c_i \ge 0$ but attains $c_i = 0$ in \emph{every} round (its shift-and-clamp gives the least-trusted client weight exactly zero, 2 of 5 clients here), so it falls outside the strict hypothesis below and is governed by Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} instead---the same distinction we draw for FLTrust's ReLU zeros in \S\ref{sec:prospective}. The strictly-positive case has a sharp answer, and it partitions the menu:

\begin{proposition}[Invariance classes under positive per-client rescaling]
\label{prop:invariance}
Let $T(u_i) = c_i u_i$ with $c_i > 0$, \textbf{strictly}---the form taken by NormClip ($c_i = \min(1, \tau/\|u_i\|)$), Reputation ($c_i \propto e^{-d_i/s}$) and RFA-reweighting ($c_i \propto 1/\max(\|u_i - \mu'\|, \varepsilon)$), each positive by construction. \textbf{FoolsGold is deliberately not in that list}: its shift-and-clamp gives the least-trusted client weight exactly $0$ in \emph{every} round (2 of 5 clients here), so a zero coefficient is not a \emph{positive} rescaling and FoolsGold-as-$d_1$ falls outside the hypothesis, governed by Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} rather than by (a)--(c). Then:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt,label=(\alph*)]
\item \textbf{Cosine similarity is exactly invariant:} $\cos(c_iu_i, c_ju_j) = \cos(u_i,u_j)$. FoolsGold's weight vector is a function of the pairwise-cosine matrix alone, so $w(T(U)) = w(U)$ identically, and \textbf{C2 holds for every $\cdot\to$FoolsGold composition}.
\item \textbf{Coordinate ordering is not invariant} in general; it survives under the bounded-ratio condition of Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(1).
\item \textbf{Consensus distance and residual magnitude are not invariant.} Heterogeneous $c_i$ can invert their ordering, so $\cdot\to$reputation compositions fail C2, and $\cdot\to$RFA needs the separation condition of Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(2).
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(a) $c_iu_i/\|c_iu_i\| = u_i/\|u_i\|$ for $c_i>0$, so every pairwise cosine, each client's normalized maximum similarity, and hence every FoolsGold weight is unchanged. (c) Counterexample for consensus distance: with $u_1{=}(1,0)$, $u_2{=}(0,1)$, $u_3{=}(3,3)$ the coordinate-wise median is $(1,1)$ and $u_3$ is farthest; under $c=(1,1,0.1)$ the median becomes $(0.3,0.3)$ and $u_3$ becomes the \emph{closest} point, reversing the ordering reputation depends on.
\end{proof}

\noindent This corrects a natural but wrong intuition. Norm clipping does \emph{not} blind FoolsGold---it cannot, by (a)---though it does blind reputation, whose signal is consensus distance rather than similarity. We verified (a) in the shipped implementation: FoolsGold's weight vector changes by at most $3\times10^{-8}$ (float32 rounding) under NormClip, across 1000 synthetic trials and live FL rounds in which 1--2 of 5 clients were genuinely clipped (raw norms up to $24.5$ against $\tau{=}5$). Note this also means Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(3)'s norm-equalizing degenerate case requires $\tau$ below \emph{all} client norms; at our $\tau{=}5$ only 1--2 of 5 clients clip per round, so norms are not equalized and that case does not describe our runs.

\subsection{Heterogeneity is not the mechanism: $\rho$ against $\Delta_c$}
\label{sec:rho_vs_deltac}

\noindent \textbf{The finding this subsection collects, stated first.} What decides whether an upstream transform costs a downstream defense its suppression is \emph{not} how heterogeneous the transform is, but \textbf{whether its reweighting is correlated with adversary identity}---and even that is not a magnitude story, because adversary-correlated reweighting has \textbf{two opposite regimes}, annihilation and attenuation, so its size alone is insufficient in both directions. This is why our own headline predictor failed, and it is the mechanistic content behind §\ref{sec:dose_response}'s refutation.

\paragraph{Two different things a coefficient vector does, and the theory sees only one of them.} (Symbols are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:notation}.) Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} turns on \textbf{dispersion}, $\rho := \max_i c_i/\min_i c_i$: how far apart the coefficients are, which is what decides whether an ordering survives. It says nothing about \emph{which} clients receive which coefficient. That second property is \textbf{adversary-correlated dispersion},
\[
\Delta_c \;:=\; \mathbb{E}[c_i \mid i \in A] \;-\; \mathbb{E}[c_i \mid i \notin A],
\qquad A \text{ the adversarial participants,}
\]
and the two are \textbf{independently controllable}---not merely uncorrelated in our sample: a transform can spread the coefficients arbitrarily ($\rho$ large) at $\Delta_c = 0$ exactly, or leave them nearly equal while shifting the adversary's share, and Proposition~\ref{prop:rho_deltac} exhibits a family of each. \textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} and Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight} constrain $\rho$; Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation}'s attenuation channel is about $\Delta_c$}, and this is the sharp form of the ``$\rho$ alone mispredicts'' point above: regimes A and B are separated by $r$, not by $\rho$. §\ref{sec:dose_outcome} finds that $\Delta_c$ is what the real upstream transforms differ on, and §\ref{sec:targeted}'s two instruments are, in retrospect, the $\Delta_c$-pinned and $\Delta_c$-swept designs. Note what $\Delta_c$ is \emph{not}: a screening quantity---computing it requires knowing $A$, which is the whole difficulty (§\ref{sec:conclusion}).

\subsection{The framework in general form}
\label{sec:general_form}

Positive rescaling is the instantiation that matters for current defenses, but the structure is more general, and stating it separates what is essential to the framework from what is an artifact of today's defense menu. Write $d_2$'s admission mechanism as a statistic $S(U)$ of the round's updates together with a decision rule that consumes only the \emph{ordering} $S$ induces over clients---which are extreme, which are far from consensus, which are down-weighted. Let $T$ be $d_1$'s per-client transform. Then $(d_1\to d_2)$ is \textbf{mechanism-preserving} exactly when
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:general_form}
S(T(U)) \;=\; \varphi\bigl(S(U)\bigr) \quad\text{for some } \varphi \text{ preserving the ordering } d_2 \text{ acts on,}
\end{equation}
which is C2; C3 additionally asks that $\varphi$ not compress the quantitative gap below $d_2$'s operating threshold. Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} is the case $T(u_i)=c_iu_i$: for $S=$ cosine similarity, $\varphi=\mathrm{id}$, so \eqref{eq:general_form} holds unconditionally; for $S=$ coordinate value, $\varphi$ is a per-client positive scaling, order-preserving only under the bounded-ratio condition $\rho < r^\star$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(1); for $S=$ consensus distance or residual magnitude, no $\varphi$ exists, because the reference point (median, geometric median) moves with $T$, so $S(T(U))$ is not a function of $S(U)$ at all. Three consequences worth stating. First, this is why the conditions are checkable without running an experiment: one reads $S$ off $d_2$'s definition and $T$ off $d_1$'s, and the question becomes algebraic. Second, it identifies what a genuinely new instantiation would require---an $S$ and a non-rescaling $T$, e.g.\ a $d_1$ that projects updates onto a subspace or applies a coordinate-wise mask, for which \eqref{eq:general_form} would need to be re-derived rather than inherited. Third, it marks the framework's real boundary: a $d_1$ whose transform is not per-client---one that mixes updates across clients, or carries cross-round state---admits no $T$ of this form, and nothing here applies to it.

\textbf{Predicted composability for our defense menu.} Criterion~\ref{thm:composability} makes precise predictions for each two-defense pair from our 7-defense menu. We validate against targeted pairs spanning all prediction categories. Key predictions:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{FoolsGold $\to$ RFA} (\textbf{PASS}, C1$\wedge$C2$\wedge$C3): FoolsGold suppresses both scaling and pixel (via similarity-based downweighting); RFA's geometric-median signal is preserved after FG's rescaling because distance ratios are maintained. Predicted: low max-ASR.
\item \textbf{Reputation $\to$ X} (\textbf{C1-FAIL for pixel}): Reputation kills scaling (ASR $0.017$) but does \emph{not} suppress pixel (baseline $0.842$). For any rep$\to$X pair, C1 fails for the pixel attack, so the criterion predicts HIGH max-committed ASR regardless of C2/C3 status. The exception is when $d_2$ independently suppresses pixel (e.g., CoordMedian partially suppresses pixel to $0.443$).
\item \textbf{NormClip $\to$ Reputation} (\textbf{C2-FAIL}): NormClip normalizes all norms to $\leq\tau$, destroying the consensus-distance signal that reputation relies on. Predicted: high ASR.
\item \textbf{FoolsGold $\to$ TrimmedMean} (\textbf{C3-FAIL}): FoolsGold downweights adversarial clients by $\alpha \ll 1$; TrimmedMean's rank signal direction is preserved (C2 holds), but rescaled adversarial coordinates $\alpha \cdot u_{a,k}$ are no longer extreme---they fall within the trimming range. Predicted: high ASR.
\item \textbf{Degenerate pairs} (FedAvg as $d_1$): $d_1$ applies no per-client transformation, so composition degenerates to $d_2$ alone.
\end{itemize}

We validate these predictions against empirical outcomes in §\ref{sec:composability_validation}.\vspace{4pt}

\textbf{Relation to the norm-based dichotomy (§\ref{sec:theory}).} The complementarity analysis we develop later (Corollary~\ref{cor:dichotomy}) shows that norm-based menus are confined to two regimes (no complementarity, or persistence-dominated realized collapse), explaining the negative result for temporal mixing. Criterion~\ref{thm:composability} generalizes that dichotomy to arbitrary defense pairs and characterizes when composition \emph{escapes} it: by using an orthogonal admission signal (C2 preserved) whose separation exceeds the threshold (C3 preserved). The next section validates the criterion's predictions.

\section{Composability Criterion Validation}
\label{sec:composability_validation}

Criterion~\ref{thm:composability} predicts for each two-defense pair whether composition achieves low max-committed ASR. All experiments in this section and §\ref{sec:fg_rfa_flagship} use one deployment regime throughout (CIFAR-10, $N{=}10$ clients, $K{=}5$ per round, adversarial fraction $f{=}0.2$, 50 rounds, 4-layer CNN), with committed attacks model\_scaling and backdoor\_pixel. \textbf{Convention:} unless stated otherwise, \emph{ASR} means \emph{max-committed ASR}---the maximum over the two committed attacks of the per-attack mean across seeds---thresholded at $0.5$---the level at which the trigger fires on the majority of triggered inputs, so that ``suppresses'' means the backdoor is not the model's dominant response; adaptive-attack, order-sensitivity and per-seed figures are labelled explicitly where they differ. A second gate is needed because a destroyed model has a low ASR for the wrong reason: every cell in §\ref{sec:dose_response}--§\ref{sec:targeted} must also hold mean clean accuracy $\ge 0.35$ (our \textbf{accuracy floor}), below which we call the cell \emph{uninterpretable} rather than suppressed. \textbf{Sample sizes are small and were fixed before the runs}: $n{=}5$ seeds per cell in the interventional suites ($n{=}8$ for \texttt{cos\_krum}, $n{=}3$ for the second-dataset replication), $n{=}30$ for the FoolsGold$\to$RFA characterization, so intervals are wide, and no null result is read as flatness unless it clears a pre-registered equivalence margin. \textbf{``Refuted'' means one thing throughout}: a pre-registered \emph{directional} hypothesis was rejected against the rule frozen for it---never that a mathematical claim was disproved. Adversaries are the fixed client identities $\{0,\dots,fN-1\}$ and each round samples $K$ participants uniformly without replacement from all $N$, with no stratification, so the per-round adversary count is \emph{hypergeometric} rather than fixed: for $N{=}10$, $f{=}0.2$, $K{=}5$, $P(n_a{=}0)=P(n_a{=}2)=56/252=0.222$ and $P(n_a{=}1)=140/252=0.556$ (measured over 100 rounds: $0.19/0.54/0.27$). Two consequences the data bear out: CoordMedian's count-majority condition $n_a < K/2 = 2.5$ holds for \emph{every} attainable $n_a\in\{0,1,2\}$, i.e.\ in 100\% of rounds, while TrimmedMean's budget $\lfloor\beta K\rfloor = \lfloor 0.2\cdot 5\rfloor = 1$ is exceeded whenever $n_a{=}2$---$22.2\%$ of rounds, ${\approx}11$ of 50. That is a second, count-based reason FoolsGold$\to$TrimmedMean fails ($0.508$) while FoolsGold$\to$CoordMedian succeeds ($0.107$) under an identical upstream transform; we retain the pre-registered C3-FAIL label, the budget violation being an additional mechanism rather than a relabelling. We validate in two waves. The \textbf{development set} (18 targeted pairs, Table~\ref{tab:composability}) spans all criterion prediction categories and was used to refine the criterion (adding C3). The \textbf{held-out set} (24 pairs, Table~\ref{tab:composability_heldout}) was \emph{pre-registered}: all 24 predictions were committed to version control (commit \texttt{7e4d9b1}) \emph{before} any held-out experiment was run, eliminating post-hoc classification concerns. The criterion predicts LOW ($<$0.5) or HIGH ($\geq$0.5) max-committed ASR. The verdict categories are:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt,leftmargin=1.5em]
\item \textbf{PASS}: C1$\wedge$C2$\wedge$C3 all hold $\Rightarrow$ predicted LOW (composition works)
\item \textbf{C1-FAIL}: neither defense individually suppresses the attack $\Rightarrow$ predicted HIGH
\item \textbf{C2-FAIL}: $d_1$ destroys $d_2$'s admission signal $\Rightarrow$ predicted HIGH
\item \textbf{C3-FAIL}: $d_1$ compresses adversarial--benign separation below $d_2$'s threshold $\Rightarrow$ predicted HIGH
\item \textbf{DEGEN}: $d_1$ has no per-client transform (trivial composition) $\Rightarrow$ predicted HIGH
\end{itemize}

\textbf{PASS precision: 100\%}---all compositions the criterion predicts to work \emph{do} work. Under the tightened per-attack C1 (§\ref{sec:composability_criterion}) the certified in-distribution set is 2 pairs, fg$\to$cm ($0.107$) and rep$\to$cm ($0.423$), both correct; fg$\to$rfa ($0.093$) is no longer certified and is reported below as a false negative of our own criterion. This is the practitioner-relevant result: the criterion's positive predictions are reliable, at the cost of 40\% recall. Overall development-set accuracy: \textbf{16/18 = 89\%}. Two misses, both in the HIGH direction:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{rfa$\to$cm} (predicted HIGH, actual 0.485) and \textbf{NC$\to$cm} (predicted HIGH, actual 0.433): CoordMedian is partially robust to boundary compression because its coordinate-wise median is invariant to monotone magnitude transformations---if $d_1$'s rescaling preserves the ordering of coordinates across clients, CoordMedian's selection is unchanged. This is exactly Proposition~\ref{prop:rank_invariance}: the C3-FAIL prediction is overly conservative for rank-based $d_2$, and both misses are the \emph{predicted} intermediate-ASR behavior of Corollary~\ref{cor:cm_robust} rather than unexplained errors. The measured quantities say how close to the uniform case these two sit: NormClip clips only $0$--$2$ of 5 clients per round (11 of 45 client-rounds over 9 live rounds), so ${\approx}76\%$ of clients receive $c_i = 1$ \emph{exactly}, and with the largest observed raw norm $24.5$ against $\tau{=}5$ the realized ratio is $\rho \leq 4.9$, which at $S{=}10$ still clears Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(1)'s bounded-ratio condition with margin $S/\rho \geq 2.0$; RFA-reweighting is likewise near-uniform across benign clients. The labelling error is therefore specifically a \emph{binary} reading of C3---``$d_1$ is heterogeneous, therefore the margin is compressed''---where the theorem asks the quantitative question $\rho < r^\star$. \textbf{The refinement does not recover either pair:} both also fail C1, since CoordMedian alone is already at $0.519$ and neither NormClip ($0.935$) nor RFA ($0.885$) suppresses. What remains is a statement about the residual---a $d_2$ whose own baseline sits at $0.519$ needs only a mild order-preserving upstream transform to fall just under $0.5$, which is what $0.433$ and $0.485$ are---and the operational consequence: evaluate C3 for rank aggregators against the realized $\rho$ from a 5--10 round pilot rather than a binary heterogeneity test, the same quantity Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight}(1) already requires and our labelling had not been using.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Per-seed spread on the headline pairs, and one number that moved.} The seed budget is not uniform across suites, so we report per-seed values and Student-$t$ 95\% intervals rather than means alone, and state each cell's $n$ wherever a composition number appears. Auditing this forced two corrections to numbers we had previously led with.

First, we topped the three headline pairs up from $n{=}3$ (seeds 42--44) to $n{=}5$ (adding 45--46). FG$\to$CM settles at $0.107$, CI $[0.064,\,0.149]$, per-seed $0.123/0.126/0.145/0.069/0.072$; Rep$\to$CM moves \emph{up} to $0.423$, CI $[0.231,\,0.616]$, per-seed $0.432/0.234/0.391/0.665/0.395$.

Second, and more consequentially, fg$\to$rfa had already been measured on \textbf{30 seeds} (42--71) as the base condition of the adaptive study in §\ref{sec:fg_rfa_flagship}. That is the largest measurement of any composition in this paper, and it gives max-committed ASR $\mathbf{0.093}$, CI $[0.062,\,0.123]$---not the $0.045$ that seeds 42--44 gave. The per-seed distribution is right-skewed (median $0.060$, mean $0.093$, sample skew $+2.07$; $8/30$ seeds at or above $0.10$; maximum $0.380$), and seeds 42--46 happen to fall in its left tail, understating the pair by a factor of $1.8$. We therefore headline the $n{=}30$ figure throughout. The $n{=}3$ value was not a computational error---it is the correct mean of those three seeds---but it was an unrepresentative sample of a skewed distribution, which is the concrete hazard in reporting 3-seed composition means and the reason we now attach an $n$ and an interval to every one. Where a seed-matched comparison is required (the adaptive-attack sweep, which ran on seeds 42--46), we quote the base on those same five seeds, $0.051 \pm 0.011$, and say so.

\textbf{One certified pair is not resolved below threshold.} Rep$\to$CM's interval $[0.231,\,0.616]$ crosses $0.5$, and one of its five seeds reaches $0.665$. Its LOW label therefore rests on a mean whose uncertainty admits the HIGH side, and we report it as correct on the mean while flagging it as the one certified pair whose margin is not statistically established. The certified in-distribution set is $2/2$ correct either way, but only fg$\to$cm is certified \emph{and} resolved below threshold at the seed budget we can afford. Clean accuracy on the three cells is $0.50$, $0.68$, $0.76$, all well clear of the collapse regime.

\paragraph{Two boundary conditions, stated alongside the primary result.} Both are developed in full later, but neither should be met late. \textbf{(i) Extreme heterogeneity breaks two of the three PASS pairs.} At $\alpha{=}0.1$, fg$\to$rfa reaches $0.569$ and rep$\to$cm $0.548$; only fg$\to$cm survives, at $0.433$. What makes this more than a negative datapoint is that the failure appears in the theorem's own quantity: RFA's separation margin $m := \Delta'_{\mathrm{sep}}/[2(R'_B+\delta')]$, measured on the transformed points, collapses from an all-round mean of $3.24$ to $1.01$---exactly the $m \to 1$ boundary at which Corollary~\ref{cor:aggregate_mass}'s bound degrades to $40\%$ adversarial mass---so a practitioner running the 5--10 round pilot would measure $m \approx 1$ and decline to deploy. We emphasise that this is a post-hoc measurement of a known failure, not a pre-registered prediction of it; full sweep and caveats (bimodality at $n{=}3$, accuracy collapse to $0.20$--$0.48$) in §\ref{sec:oos_pass}(E). \textbf{(ii) Mechanism preservation is not adaptive-attack robustness.} A criterion-aware adversary solving FoolsGold's and RFA's constraints jointly---decorrelated coordinate blocks to evade the similarity test, $\epsilon$-ball projection to stay inside the geometric median's radius---raises fg$\to$rfa from $0.051$ (seed-matched) to $0.167$ within our evaluated composition-aware attack family (§\ref{sec:fg_rfa_flagship}). Theorem~\ref{thm:bounded_reweight} bounds what the preserved mechanism admits, not what an adversary reshaping its update to satisfy both constraints at once can do, and the $3.3\times$ increase is the size of that gap on our flagship pair.

\textbf{Held-out validation (pre-registered): 22/24 = 92\%} (\emph{Screening}: an evaluation ordering, and by Proposition~\ref{prop:identification} not an identification of the mechanism's contribution)\textbf{.} Table~\ref{tab:composability_heldout} reports the 24 pre-registered pairs. Combined across both waves: \textbf{38/42 = 90.5\%}. The two held-out misses are both LOW-predicted pairs that were flagged as \emph{speculative} in the pre-registration (neither is a clean C1$\wedge$C2$\wedge$C3 PASS):
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{fedavg$\to$cm} (predicted LOW, actual 0.519): a degenerate pair reducing to CoordMedian alone, whose max single-defense ASR (0.450 at $n{=}3$) sat just below threshold but rose to 0.519 at $n{=}5$---a borderline crossing, not a mechanism failure.
\item \textbf{fg$\to$NC} (predicted LOW, actual 0.927): the one genuine miss. We speculated FoolsGold's downweighting plus NormClip's bound would combine; in fact NormClip adds no suppression (it only clips norms, which FoolsGold has already equalized) and FoolsGold's partial downweighting alone is insufficient. This is a C1 failure we mis-scored as a hopeful LOW.
\end{itemize}
Critically, \emph{every composition the tightened criterion certifies is correct} (2/2, both in the development set); the held-out misses are speculative LOW calls the criterion itself does not license, and fg$\to$rfa---which works but no longer certifies---is reported above as a synergy false negative.

All predictions committed to version control (commit \texttt{7e4d9b1}) before experiments. Threshold: LOW $< 0.5$, HIGH $\geq 0.5$. Only the two LOW predictions are shown individually; the 22 HIGH predictions (all correct) are summarized.}
\label{tab:composability_heldout}

\textbf{Rep$\to$tm: composition dominates despite C1-FAIL.} Reputation$\to$TrimmedMean is correctly classified as C1-FAIL (pixel ASR 0.679 $> 0.5$), yet it is the paper's main composition result (§\ref{sec:composition_rep_tm}). The resolution: the criterion predicts \emph{max-committed ASR}, while the deployment-relevant metric is \emph{oracle ASR} (0.316). The oracle cannot exploit the pixel gap because scaling---which reputation \emph{does} suppress---dominates its utility. The criterion thus correctly identifies that rep$\to$tm does not suppress every committed attack, while the game-theoretic analysis shows it is strategically dominant.

\textbf{Tightening C1 costs us our own flagship---and reveals a synergy case.} Under the per-attack quantifier in C1, \textbf{fg$\to$rfa no longer certifies}: neither FoolsGold ($0.732$) nor RFA ($0.845$) suppresses the pixel backdoor individually, and FoolsGold's $0.200$ against model-scaling is hollow because it comes with $10\%$ clean accuracy. The composition nonetheless reaches $0.093$. This is genuine \emph{synergy}---suppression neither constituent possesses---and Proposition~\ref{prop:emergent} proves that \emph{no} inherited-suppression screen can certify it, so this is a scope boundary rather than a tuning failure. We therefore report our lowest-ASR composition as a false negative of our own criterion. The certified in-distribution set is fg$\to$cm ($0.107$) and rep$\to$cm ($0.423$), both correct: precision $2/2$, recall $2/5 = 40\%$.

\subsection{A prospective test on a never-run defense menu}
\label{sec:prospective}

Everything above shares a structural weakness: the conditions were developed on the same 7-defense menu they are scored on, and every genuinely positive test is a PASS prediction. The decisive test is therefore a \emph{prospective} one---labels frozen and committed before any run, on pairs no condition was ever tuned against. Our server implements three defenses the paper had never used: FLTrust~\citep{cao2021fltrust}, Krum and Multi-Krum~\citep{blanchard2017machine}. They are well chosen because the theory makes \emph{opposite} predictions about them. FLTrust applies a per-client rescaling $c_i = \mathrm{ReLU}(\cos(u_i, g_{\mathrm{srv}}))\cdot\|g_{\mathrm{srv}}\|/\|u_i\| \ge 0$ (verified in the implementation: all scales non-negative, cosine preserved to $2.4\times10^{-7}$). Note the hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} is $c_i > 0$, strictly: \textbf{FLTrust is positive-rescaling when its trust coefficient is strictly positive, and zero-trust clients require the separate zero-weight analysis} of Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation}. On the strictly-positive part, Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}(a) predicts $\cdot\to$FLTrust preserves C2; on the ReLU zeros, FLTrust changes the effective client set rather than only magnitudes, which is Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} and not covered by (a). The distinction is not cosmetic here: it is exactly what separates the five bit-identical $\cdot\to$FLTrust pairs below from FG$\to$FLTrust, the one $d_1$ that produces exact zeros and the one that genuinely deviates. Krum and Multi-Krum select on \emph{pairwise Euclidean distance}---the non-invariant class of Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}(c)---so $\cdot\to$Krum is predicted to fail C2, while $\mathrm{Krum}\to\cdot$ is degenerate because a selection rule emits no per-client transform.

\textbf{Protocol.} We recorded a predicted label and a mechanism for all 20 pairs in \texttt{pre\_registration\_prospective.md} and git-committed it (\texttt{262cf35}) before \texttt{results/prospective\_suite/} existed, then ran $20$ pairs $\times\,2$ committed attacks $\times\,3$ seeds $\times\,50$ rounds in the standard configuration. Predictions were never revised. Single-defense baselines for the three new defenses were measured \emph{before} the freeze, since C0 and C1 take them as inputs. Crucially, the suite carries the sanity gate the DBA battery lacked: a predicted-FAIL control counts only if the attack clears ASR $0.5$ against FedAvg, and both do here ($0.667$ model-scaling, $0.768$ pixel). Unlike the out-of-sample battery of §\ref{sec:oos_pass}, these negatives are informative.

\textbf{Result: 13/20, and the shortfall is in our experimental design.} At the same $0.5$ threshold: TP${}=8$, FP${}=0$, TN${}=5$, FN${}=7$ (Table~\ref{tab:prospective}). Every predicted-LOW pair came out LOW, $8/8$---and that figure is far weaker than it looks, because \textbf{FLTrust suppresses both committed attacks on its own} (max-committed ASR $0.048$ at $0.575$ clean accuracy), the first defense in our menu that does. All 13 pairs containing FLTrust land in $[0.041, 0.078]$, within $0.030$ of FLTrust alone, in either slot. The two slots fail for different reasons, and the distinction matters:

\textbf{The two slots fail differently, and the difference is the finding.} \emph{(i) FLTrust as $d_1$ (7 pairs, 2/7).} $d_1$ alone already suppresses, so the composition inherits that suppression whatever $d_2$ does; all five predicted-HIGH misses have real C2/C3 failures, but destroying $d_2$'s mechanism is costless when $d_2$ was never load-bearing. This is C0, invisible on the original 42 pairs because no defense there suppresses alone (the minimum single-defense max-committed ASR is CoordMedian's $0.519$, and the lowest ASR among all 39 non-PASS pairs is $0.433$), so we count these 7 pairs out of scope \emph{retrospectively}---a weaker claim than a pre-registered exclusion. \emph{(ii) FLTrust as $d_2$ (6 pairs, 6/6), but the prediction is nearly an identity.} FLTrust gates on the scale-invariant $\cos(u_i, g_{\mathrm{srv}})$ and then renormalizes every update to $\|g_{\mathrm{srv}}\|$, discarding magnitude entirely, so a positive per-client rescaling is \emph{exactly} cancelled---four of the six pairs are bit-identical to FLTrust alone or differ only within seed noise. The single genuine deviation is FG$\to$FLTrust ($0.064$), the one $d_1$ producing exact \emph{zeros} rather than positive rescalings and hence a Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} interaction. This arm is a consistency check between theory and implementation, \textbf{not} a falsifiable test: every $d_1$ in our menu is a positive rescaling and Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}(a) permits no other outcome. \emph{(iii) Neither defense is FLTrust (7 pairs, 5/7)}---the only arm that could have surprised us. Five predicted-HIGH pairs are correct ($0.870$--$0.943$); both misses are diagnosable and neither is a mechanism error: fg$\to$krum measures $0.078$ at $9.9\%$ clean accuracy (the collapsed-model artifact, not suppression), and krum$\to$cm measures $0.450$ against a $0.5$ threshold as a degenerate pair reducing to CoordMedian alone, whose own baseline is $0.519$. The arm contains \emph{zero} predicted-LOW pairs, so it bears on specificity only. Per-pair numbers for all three arms are in Appendix~\ref{app:prospective_arms}.

\textbf{What the suite does and does not establish.} Counting only in-scope pairs gives $11/13$; the arm that carried real risk of falsification gives $5/7$ with both misses explained. But we will not present this as the prospective positive result the framework needs, because \textbf{it does not contain one}: the 8 predicted-LOW pairs are 6 near-identities plus 2 out-of-scope inheritances. The three matched contrasts holding $d_1$ fixed while varying $d_2$'s invariance class---rep$\to$fltrust $0.046$ vs.\ rep$\to$krum $0.880$; nc$\to$fltrust $0.046$ vs.\ nc$\to$mkrum $0.943$; rfa$\to$fltrust $0.047$ vs.\ rfa$\to$mkrum $0.875$---show a $19$--$20\times$ ASR gap turning on exactly the property Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} identifies, which is the most suggestive evidence here. They are still confounded: in this menu the invariant-signal $d_2$ is also the only $d_2$ that suppresses alone, so C1 and C2 co-vary. Lemma~\ref{lem:testability} explains why no other choice of defense would have helped---in scope, C0$\wedge$C1 \emph{force} $d_2$ to suppress $a^*$ alone, so the confounding is structural. Breaking it requires holding the suppression principle and standalone effectiveness fixed while varying only the invariance class, which is the design of §\ref{sec:metric_swap}.

\subsection{Breaking the confound: a matched metric-swap ablation}
\label{sec:metric_swap}

Lemma~\ref{lem:testability} says mechanism--effectiveness confounding cannot be escaped by picking a different defense off a menu: in scope, C0$\wedge$C1 force the invariant-signal $d_2$ to be one that suppresses $a^*$ alone. So we break the confound by construction instead, holding the suppression \emph{principle} fixed and varying only the invariance class of the statistic $d_2$ reads.

\textbf{Construction.} Two downstream defenses in our menu score clients by a Euclidean quantity---Reputation weights by distance to the client median, Krum selects by summed pairwise distance---and for each we build a minimal-change mirror computing the same score from \emph{directions} alone. \texttt{cos\_reputation} normalizes each update first ($\hat u_i = u_i/\max(\|u_i\|,\varepsilon)$), takes the coordinate-wise median of the $\hat u_i$ as consensus, and scores $d_i = 1 - \cos(u_i, \mathrm{consensus})$ with bandwidth $\sigma = \mathrm{median}_i(d_i)$ and weights $w_i \propto \exp(-d_i/\sigma)$; normalizing \emph{first} is what makes it invariant, since Reputation's reference is the \emph{client} median, which itself moves when $d_1$ rescales. \texttt{cos\_krum} replaces the pairwise Euclidean distance matrix with $1-\cos(u_i,u_j)$, invariant in \emph{both} arguments, so the score matrix and the selected index are unchanged under any positive per-client rescaling. Everything else is identical to the original: same soft-trim form, same adaptive median bandwidth, same $\arg\min$ rule, no clipping, no memory across rounds.

\textbf{These are mechanism-isolating ablations, not proposed defenses.} They are the analogue of a knockout control: their only purpose is to vary one property while holding the rest fixed. We do not evaluate their standalone merit beyond the C1 input the design requires, and we do not recommend them.

\textbf{Why this design is falsifiable where the FLTrust contrasts were not.} Both mirrors fall under Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance}(a) in its general form---any statistic that is a function of the directions $\hat u_i$ alone is exactly invariant under $T(u_i)=c_iu_i$, $c_i>0$---while their Euclidean originals fall under (c). Taking $d_1 \in \{$NormClip, RFA$\}$, both heterogeneous positive rescalings and neither suppressing model-scaling alone ($0.935$, $0.885$, so C0 holds and the composition cannot inherit suppression from $d_1$), each control/test pair at fixed $d_1$ was intended to differ in exactly one property: whether C2 holds. That requires the C1 input to be matched across the pair, so we measured it first, before freezing any label. In §\ref{sec:prospective} the two changes were entangled---``different invariance class'' and ``different defense'' were the same edit, and FLTrust suppressed both committed attacks alone, so a confirmation could not have come out otherwise. Here neither $d_2$ is a universal suppressor, so no outcome can be inherited from $d_1$ or from $d_2$ alone, and if the invariant arm also lands HIGH then C2's invariance mechanism is refuted with no appeal to a different defense available. Both committed attacks are run: on the pixel backdoor C1 fails for every pair, so all pixel cells are predicted HIGH---a negative control the framework must get right---and the sanity gate holds (FedAvg reaches $0.667$ on scaling, $0.768$ on pixel), so predicted-HIGH cells are informative rather than vacuous as in the DBA battery.

\textbf{The design's premise was refuted by its own baselines.} Matching failed in both arms, for two independent reasons, and because the C1 inputs are measured before the freeze we learned this before the suite ran---the only reason it can be reported as a finding rather than a post-hoc excuse. \texttt{cos\_reputation} does not merely weaken relative to its Euclidean original, it collapses, from Reputation's $0.017$ on model-scaling to $\mathbf{0.982}$. \texttt{cos\_krum} reaches $0.078$ on model-scaling, which passes C1's numeric threshold, but at $\mathbf{0.146}$ clean accuracy---the hollow-suppression artifact already documented for FoolsGold ($0.200$ at $0.10$) and fg$\to$krum ($0.078$ at $0.099$)---so C1 fails under the accuracy gate we apply everywhere else. The mechanism is visible in the baselines rather than asserted: undefended FedAvg under model-scaling is itself at $0.100$ accuracy, so Krum's $0.577$ is \emph{restoring} utility the attack destroyed and \texttt{cos\_krum}'s $0.146$ fails to. \textbf{Magnitude information is what lets this defense family handle a magnitude attack}: a direction-only mirror of a Euclidean scorer cannot see a scaling attack at all, so removing the magnitude dependence removes the suppression with it---exactly the coupling the pre-registered contingency anticipated, stated there as the possibility that ``invariance and standalone effectiveness may be inherently coupled'' for this family. On the magnitude-neutral pixel backdoor the mirror does survive ($0.300$ at $0.598$), but there its control Krum fails C1 ($0.583$), so those arms are unmatched in the opposite direction and the pixel cells cannot carry the test either.

The consequence is that \textbf{the confound re-materialized inside an experiment built specifically to break it}, which we report as the round's methodological finding rather than as a failed run. Lemma~\ref{lem:testability} establishes that mechanism--effectiveness confounding is structural in scope; this is what trying to escape it looks like when the mechanism being varied is the mechanism the defense relies on. Two responses were available and we took neither. We did not switch to a pixel-only protocol, which would have made \texttt{cos\_krum} pass C1 and manufactured two predicted-LOW cells---choosing the attack after seeing the C1 inputs is attack-shopping regardless of the resulting labels. We did not adjust the accuracy gate to admit $0.146$, which would have produced a predicted-LOW pair by weakening the very criterion that catches hollow suppression elsewhere. The pre-registration's second non-negotiable---``no switching of committed attack, threshold, seed count, or accuracy gate''---forecloses both, and either would have bought a positive test at the cost of the property that makes the test worth running.

\textbf{The mechanism is verified in code before it is claimed.} We check each $d_2$'s internal state on $\{u_i\}$ against $\{c_iu_i\}$ for the $c_i$ NormClip and RFA actually produce on live rounds, rather than inferring invariance from an ASR. Over 18 rounds \texttt{cos\_reputation}'s weight \emph{ordering} never changes ($0/18$, weights agreeing to $\le 2.5\times10^{-7}$) and \texttt{cos\_krum} selects the same client in $18/18$, while their Euclidean originals change in $12/18$ and $6/18$; the disturbance tracks the realized $\rho$ exactly as Proposition~\ref{prop:invariance} predicts. Full per-upstream rates, the 1000-draw synthetic check, and the agreement of the mirrors with the shipped implementations are in Appendix~\ref{app:metric_swap_detail}.

One asymmetry belongs in the statement of the prediction. \texttt{cos\_reputation}'s \emph{weight vector} is exactly invariant, but its aggregate is not, because the weighted average is taken over the rescaled updates $c_iu_i$. That residual is a magnitude contraction of the Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} kind, so the prediction is two-sided: ASR should stay low while clean accuracy pays a contraction cost. \texttt{cos\_krum} has no such residual---it emits one selected update, and the selection is bit-identical.

\textbf{Outcome: 8/8 correct, and zero positive tests.} Because C1 binds for all eight pairs---on model-scaling through the two baseline failures above, on the pixel backdoor by design---every frozen label was HIGH, so the suite bears on specificity only, as a consequence of inputs measured before the freeze rather than a choice made afterwards. We ran all 8 pairs $\times$ 2 attacks $\times$ 3 seeds anyway, because a suite whose labels are all one class is still falsifiable in the direction that matters: a single LOW cell would have been a false positive against a criterion claiming $100\%$ precision. All eight came out HIGH---$8/8$ correct, TP $=0$, FP $=0$, TN $=8$, FN $=0$, max-committed ASR spanning $0.508$--$0.935$---with labels frozen in \texttt{pre\_registration\_metric\_swap.md} at commit \texttt{356897b}, timestamped before \texttt{results/metric\_swap/} existed and reproduced verbatim in the runner's \texttt{PAIRS} list so scoring cannot drift. We state the limitation in the same breath as the result: the suite contains \textbf{no falsifiable prospective positive test}, and $8/8$ is not validation of the framework's predictive claim---eight correct negatives on pairs our own conditions could only have called HIGH is weak evidence, and it supports a claim about specificity, not prediction.

One prediction in the suite was genuinely two-sided, and it is the round's only prospective positive result. \texttt{cos\_krum}'s C1 verdict on model-scaling turns entirely on whether the accuracy gate is applied ($0.078$ passes the numeric threshold; $0.146$ accuracy fails the gate), so rather than choose a reading afterwards we froze \emph{both}, with the resolution rule fixed in advance: accuracy-gated $\Rightarrow$ HIGH, numeric-only $\Rightarrow$ LOW, and a LOW outcome at accuracy $\ge 0.35$ would have recorded our two primary labels as misses. Both pairs came out \textbf{HIGH} (NormClip$\to$\texttt{cos\_krum} $0.508$, RFA$\to$\texttt{cos\_krum} $0.566$), refuting the secondary reading and vindicating the accuracy gate against a pre-registered alternative specification of itself on data neither of us had seen. The weaker half is flagged in Appendix~\ref{app:metric_swap_detail}: the NormClip arm sits barely above threshold and it is the RFA arm that carries the conclusion.

\textbf{A post-hoc contrast that is not confounded.} The cross-pair scoring above is confounded in exactly the way Lemma~\ref{lem:testability} predicts, but the frozen suite happens to contain a comparison that is not, and it is the strongest C2 evidence in either paper. Compare a composition against \emph{the same} $d_2$'s own standalone ASR on the same attack, under two different upstream transforms. C1 is then identical by construction---same downstream defense, same attack, same measured standalone effectiveness on both sides---so the residual $\Delta = \mathrm{ASR}(d_1{\to}d_2, a) - \mathrm{ASR}(d_2, a)$ cannot be attributed to $d_2$ being a stronger or weaker defense. What does vary is how much the upstream transform actually disturbs $d_2$'s statistic, and that quantity was measured on the mechanism itself (§\ref{sec:metric_swap}, \texttt{results/cos\_invariance\_check.json}) rather than inferred from any ASR. The frozen label scoring is fixed at $n{=}3$ and must not move, so we topped the six powered cells up to $n{=}5$ in a separate directory, with the tested direction fixed in the top-up script before the new seeds ran (\texttt{results/metric\_swap\_topup/}). For Krum under model-scaling (standalone $0.061$ at $0.577$ accuracy): NormClip changes Krum's selection in $0/9$ rounds and the composition reaches $0.062$ ($\Delta = +0.001$, per-seed $0.120 / 0.018 / 0.047 / 0.058 / 0.068$); RFA changes it in $6/9$ rounds and the composition reaches $0.779$ ($\Delta = +0.718$, per-seed $0.974 / 0.116 / 0.982 / 0.904 / 0.916$)---a gap of $+0.716$ between two compositions sharing a downstream defense, an attack, and a baseline, and at $n{=}5$ it is statistically resolved (Welch $t = 4.28$, two-sided $p = 0.012$; paired $t = 4.60$, $p = 0.010$; Mann--Whitney one-sided $p = 0.008$), which it was not at $n{=}3$.

The pattern holds across the suite once cells without power are excluded---$\Delta$ is observable only where standalone $d_2$ genuinely suppresses, since a baseline already above $0.5$ shows $\Delta \approx 0$ by ceiling effect whatever the transform did. Restricting to standalone ASR $< 0.5$ at accuracy $\ge 0.35$ leaves six cells, and at $n{=}5$ measured disturbance still splits them perfectly: the three undisturbed cells ($0/9$) give $\Delta \in [+0.001, +0.190]$, the three disturbed ones ($>0/9$) give $\Delta \in [+0.718, +0.878]$---a separation of $+0.527$ with no overlap. Two of the three undisturbed cells are \texttt{cos\_krum} on the pixel backdoor, where the mirror suppresses standalone ($0.300$ at $0.598$ accuracy) and carries that suppression through under \emph{both} upstreams. Here the two extra seeds \emph{weaken} the confirming half rather than strengthen it, and we report it as measured: $\Delta$ moves from $+0.000$ at $n{=}3$ to $+0.190$ under NormClip and from $-0.027$ to $+0.147$ under RFA, leaving both compositions still below threshold ($0.490$ and $0.447$ at $0.61$ accuracy) but no longer intact. Exact invariance of the \emph{selection} therefore does not by itself carry suppression through unchanged: the magnitude channel of Lemma~\ref{lem:annihilation} is a second route by which $\Delta$ can grow, and the ladder's arm 3 (§\ref{sec:dose_response}) is built to measure exactly that prospectively rather than describe it post-hoc. The ten excluded cells are listed rather than dropped silently in Appendix~\ref{app:metric_swap_detail}.

The suite contains a second such contrast, on Reputation under model-scaling (standalone $0.017$ at $0.777$ accuracy), and the two are informative in opposite ways. NormClip disturbs Reputation's weight ordering in $3/9$ rounds and the composition reaches $0.806$; RFA disturbs it in $9/9$ and the composition reaches $0.895$. The gap is only $+0.089$---both arms are far above threshold, because Reputation's ordering is disturbed under \emph{both} upstreams---but within-arm variance is small, all five seeds move in the predicted direction (per-seed deltas $+0.149 / +0.089 / +0.030 / +0.061 / +0.115$), and it is resolved at both $n{=}3$ and $n{=}5$ (Welch $t = 3.06$, two-sided $p = 0.018$; paired $t = 4.31$, $p = 0.013$). So the two contrasts trade off exactly as the disturbance measurements imply: Krum has the large effect because one of its upstreams leaves it untouched, and Reputation was resolvable even at $n{=}3$ because neither upstream does.

\textbf{This is post-hoc, and we do not present it otherwise.} It was not pre-registered, it re-uses a suite frozen for a different purpose, the two extra seeds per cell were added \emph{after} seeing the $n{=}3$ result, and the six-cell split is a description of that suite rather than a test on new data. The top-up cut in both directions, which is why we report all of it: it resolved the Krum gap (Welch $p = 0.16$ at $n{=}3$, where per-seed paired deltas $+0.854 / +0.097 / +0.935$ left one seed not separating, to $p = 0.012$ at $n{=}5$) and at the same time degraded the invariant control's $\Delta$ from $\approx 0$ to $+0.19$. What the analysis does have, which neither the FLTrust contrasts of §\ref{sec:prospective} nor the cross-pair scoring has, is C1 held exactly fixed and a disturbance measurement taken on the statistic rather than read back from the outcome. We offer it as the cleanest available piece of C2 evidence and as the shape a properly prospective version of this test would take: fix a downstream defense that suppresses the attack, then vary the upstream along measured disturbance rather than along defense identity. Table~\ref{tab:can_cannot} sets out, claim by claim, what this design can and cannot conclude.

\label{sec:condition_scoring}
